People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment was… I’ve been having these thoughts, like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. I don’t mean I want to hurt myself—because I don’t—but it scared me that my mind even goes there.

I almost didn’t call, actually. I kept telling myself, “Oh, you’re just tired,” and then I’d get busy with work stuff, schedules and customers and all that, and put it off. But it’s been sitting with me, and I thought I should probably talk to someone before I just keep brushing it aside.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still try—I’ll set out my paints at home, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because I always liked that. But then I get there and I just sort of… stare at the clay, or the brushes, and I don’t feel that spark anymore.

It’s strange because I used to lose track of time with that stuff. And now I’ll start thinking about errands or whether I forgot to schedule someone properly at the store, and then suddenly I’m just sitting there doing nothing. So yes, I guess I’m not really enjoying those things the way I used to.

carrying groceries in from the car.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying little mistakes at work, like a display sign being crooked or a trainee looking confused, and I start telling myself I’m a useless supervisor.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: Decisions that used to be automatic, like choosing which endcap to reset first or what blouse to wear, can leave me standing there foggy and second-guessing myself.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of tomorrow, that might be easier,' even though I don’t want to hurt myself.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: Before this slump hit, I had a stretch where I felt almost too sure of myself and then would snap at people over tiny comments, especially when my district manager questioned my layouts.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: For a while I had this burst of energy where I couldn’t sit still after work and kept sketching store ideas, reorganizing shelves at home, and signing up for extra training I didn’t really need.\n- key: S035\n was convinced I could fix the whole store’s image myself and that the district manager just couldn’t see how brilliant my ideas were.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: color, a song on the speaker, or a new idea popping into my head.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I was emailing coworkers after hours, proposing new cultural promotion displays, offering to mentor extra staff, and filling notebook I think… about six months? It’s hard to pin down exactly, because at first I kept blaming it on being busy at the store or the weather, you know, when it was too hot to take my morning walk and then suddenly it was raining every other day.

But I can get stuck on it and start telling myself I’m just… useless at my job.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with these thoughts and lack of enjoyment in activities?\n\nPatient: I think… about six months? It’s hard to pin down exactly, because at first I kept blaming it on being busy at the store or the weather, you know, when it was too hot to take my morning walk and then suddenly it was raining every other day.\n\nBut around six months is when I noticed the flatness wasn’t really lifting. The art classes, the walks, those little routines that usually made me feel like myself… they just stopped doing that. I’d still go sometimes, but it didn’t feel restorative, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. It comes up… regularly, I guess. Not every minute or anything, and I’m not making plans to hurt myself, but it’s enough that it scared me.

I kept telling myself, “Oh, you’re just tired,” and then I’d get busy with work stuff, schedules and customers and all that, and put it off. But it’s been sitting with me, and I thought I should probably talk to someone before I just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: I’m not exactly sure when it started in a clear way. It’s more that it’s been creeping in lately, like I’ll be lying there or just worn out after a day, and the thought pops up—if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nI don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t. That’s part of why it scared me. I’m usually the one keeping things moving, you know, at work and everything, and then to have that thought show up… it made me think I shouldn’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still try—I’ll set out my paints at home, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because I always liked that. But then I get there and I just sort of… stare at the clay, or the brushes, and I don’t feel that spark anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to lose track of time with that stuff. And now I’ll start thinking about errands or whether I forgot to schedule someone properly at the store, and then suddenly I’m just sitting there doing nothing. So yes, I guess I’m not really enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem about the same, I guess.\n\nThough, honestly, I avoid the scale sometimes because then I start thinking about everything else I should be doing—meal planning, walking more, all of that—and then I end up reorganizing a drawer or making a list for the store schedule instead. But no, nothing I’ve really noticed weight-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any real way. I go to bed, and then sometimes I start thinking about what I didn’t finish around the house or little things from work, but I don’t know if that counts as “trouble sleeping” or just… being me.\n\nI guess I’d have to pay more attention to it. I can’t really say for sure right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep thinking I will, like with painting or pottery. I’ll even set out my paints sometimes, or tell myself, “Go to the class, you always liked the class.” But then I’m there and I just sort of look at the clay or the brushes, and nothing really… catches.\n\nI used to get so absorbed in it. I’d forget to eat lunch if I was working on something, which drove my sister crazy because she’d say I was impossible to make plans with. Now it’s more like I’m going through the motions. So I guess there isn’t much I really look forward to the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, over little things mostly.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think both, actually, which sounds strange when I say it.

Some days I catch myself pacing around the living room, kind of going back and forth for no real reason, and picking at my cuticles. I’ll realize I’ve been doing it while the TV is on, but I haven’t watched a single thing. My sister used to do that during football games—well, not the cuticles, she’d tap her foot so hard it drove everyone crazy—but anyway.

Other days it’s the opposite. Like at work, walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels… weirdly slow, like my body just won’t move at a normal pace. Not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.

But the thought has been coming up… like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.\n\nAnd then I get scared that I even thought it, honestly. I’ll realize I’ve been doing it while the TV is on, but I haven’t watched a single thing. My sister used to do that during football games—well, not the cuticles, she’d tap her foot so hard it drove everyone crazy—but anyway.\n\nOther days it’s the opposite. Not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant change in how your clothes fit over the past six months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 62 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Yes, I do. Some days I’ll realize I’ve been pacing around the living room, just back and forth, and picking at my cuticles without really meaning to.

I’m usually the one keeping things moving, you know, at work and everything, and then to have that thought show up… it made me think I shouldn’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment was… I’ve been having these thoughts, like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. I don’t mean I want to hurt myself—because I don’t—but it scared me that my mind even goes there.\n\nI almost didn’t call, actually. I kept telling myself, “Oh, you’re just tired,” and then I’d get busy with work stuff, schedules and customers and all that, and put it off. But it’s been sitting with me, and I thought I should probably talk to someone before I just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: I’m not exactly sure when it started in a clear way. It’s more that it’s been creeping in lately, like I’ll be lying there or just worn out after a day, and the thought pops up—if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nI don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t. That’s part of why it scared me. I’m usually the one keeping things moving, you know, at work and everything, and then to have that thought show up… it made me think I shouldn’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still try—I’ll set out my paints at home, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because I always liked that. But then I get there and I just sort of… stare at the clay, or the brushes, and I don’t feel that spark anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to lose track of time with that stuff. And now I’ll start thinking about errands or whether I forgot to schedule someone properly at the store, and then suddenly I’m just sitting there doing nothing. So yes, I guess I’m not really enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem about the same, I guess.\n\nThough, honestly, I avoid the scale sometimes because then I start thinking about everything else I should be doing—meal planning, walking more, all of that—and then I end up reorganizing a drawer or making a list for the store schedule instead. But no, nothing I’ve really noticed weight-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any real way. I go to bed, and then sometimes I start thinking about what I didn’t finish around the house or little things from work, but I don’t know if that counts as “trouble sleeping” or just… being me.\n\nI guess I’d have to pay more attention to it. I can’t really say for sure right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep thinking I will, like with painting or pottery. I’ll even set out my paints sometimes, or tell myself, “Go to the class, you always liked the class.” But then I’m there and I just sort of look at the clay or the brushes, and nothing really… catches.\n\nI used to get so absorbed in it. I’d forget to eat lunch if I was working on something, which drove my sister crazy because she’d say I was impossible to make plans with. Now it’s more like I’m going through the motions. So I guess there isn’t much I really look forward to the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, over little things mostly. Like at work, if a display sign is a little crooked, or one of the trainees looks confused after I’ve explained something, I’ll keep replaying it later.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, “What kind of supervisor am I if I can’t even make that clear?” It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because it’s not like the store fell apart or anything. But I can get stuck on it and start telling myself I’m just… useless at my job.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with these thoughts and lack of enjoyment in activities?\n\nPatient: I think… about six months? It’s hard to pin down exactly, because at first I kept blaming it on being busy at the store or the weather, you know, when it was too hot to take my morning walk and then suddenly it was raining every other day.\n\nBut around six months is when I noticed the flatness wasn’t really lifting. The art classes, the walks, those little routines that usually made me feel like myself… they just stopped doing that. I’d still go sometimes, but it didn’t feel restorative, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed in any big way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, though. I used to keep a scale in the bathroom, but then it started giving weird numbers after my niece’s kids were jumping around in there one weekend—well, they weren’t on the scale exactly, but you know how kids are. So I can’t say for certain. But significant weight loss? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not waking up more than once or twice, or have they been coming up regularly?\n\nPatient: It’s been more than once or twice, yes. Not like I’m sitting there making plans or anything—I don’t want to hurt myself. But the thought has been coming up… like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.\n\nAnd then I get scared that I even thought it, honestly. That’s part of why I finally made this appointment. I kept telling myself it was just stress, like when inventory gets backed up and everyone’s asking me where things are, and you just want to disappear for five minutes—but this felt different. It kept coming back.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these thoughts about not waking up come to you?\n\nPatient: It comes up… regularly, I guess. Not every minute or anything, and I’m not making plans to hurt myself, but it’s enough that it scared me.\n\nIt’ll be this thought like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And then I think, why did I even think that? You know, I’ll be doing something ordinary, like getting ready for work or sitting after dinner, and it sort of drifts in. That’s really what finally pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think both, actually, which sounds strange when I say it.\n\nSome days I catch myself pacing around the living room, kind of going back and forth for no real reason, and picking at my cuticles. I’ll realize I’ve been doing it while the TV is on, but I haven’t watched a single thing. My sister used to do that during football games—well, not the cuticles, she’d tap her foot so hard it drove everyone crazy—but anyway.\n\nOther days it’s the opposite. Like at work, walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels… weirdly slow, like my body just won’t move at a normal pace. Not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in how your clothes fit over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment was… I’ve been having these thoughts, like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. I kept telling myself, “Oh, you’re just tired,” and then I’d get busy with work stuff, schedules and customers and all that, and put it off. But it’s been sitting with me, and I thought I should probably talk to someone before I just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: I’m not exactly sure when it started in a clear way. It’s more that it’s been creeping in lately, like I’ll be lying there or just worn out after a day, and the thought pops up—if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nI don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t. That’s part of why it scared me. I’m usually the one keeping things moving, you know, at work and everything, and then to have that thought show up… it made me think I shouldn’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still try—I’ll set out my paints at home, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because I always liked that. But then I get there and I just sort of… stare at the clay, or the brushes, and I don’t feel that spark anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to lose track of time with that stuff. And now I’ll start thinking about errands or whether I forgot to schedule someone properly at the store, and then suddenly I’m just sitting there doing nothing. So yes, I guess I’m not really enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem about the same, I guess.\n\nThough, honestly, I avoid the scale sometimes because then I start thinking about everything else I should be doing—meal planning, walking more, all of that—and then I end up reorganizing a drawer or making a list for the store schedule instead. But no, nothing I’ve really noticed weight-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any real way. I go to bed, and then sometimes I start thinking about what I didn’t finish around the house or little things from work, but I don’t know if that counts as “trouble sleeping” or just… being me.\n\nI guess I’d have to pay more attention to it. I can’t really say for sure right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep thinking I will, like with painting or pottery. I’ll even set out my paints sometimes, or tell myself, “Go to the class, you always liked the class.” But then I’m there and I just sort of look at the clay or the brushes, and nothing really… catches.\n\nI used to get so absorbed in it. I’d forget to eat lunch if I was working on something, which drove my sister crazy because she’d say I was impossible to make plans with. Now it’s more like I’m going through the motions. So I guess there isn’t much I really look forward to the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, over little things mostly. Like at work, if a display sign is a little crooked, or one of the trainees looks confused after I’ve explained something, I’ll keep replaying it later.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, “What kind of supervisor am I if I can’t even make that clear?” It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because it’s not like the store fell apart or anything. But I can get stuck on it and start telling myself I’m just… useless at my job.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with these thoughts and lack of enjoyment in activities?\n\nPatient: I think… about six months? It’s hard to pin down exactly, because at first I kept blaming it on being busy at the store or the weather, you know, when it was too hot to take my morning walk and then suddenly it was raining every other day.\n\nBut around six months is when I noticed the flatness wasn’t really lifting. The art classes, the walks, those little routines that usually made me feel like myself… they just stopped doing that. I’d still go sometimes, but it didn’t feel restorative, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed in any big way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, though. I used to keep a scale in the bathroom, but then it started giving weird numbers after my niece’s kids were jumping around in there one weekend—well, they weren’t on the scale exactly, but you know how kids are. So I can’t say for certain. But significant weight loss? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not waking up more than once or twice, or have they been coming up regularly?\n\nPatient: It’s been more than once or twice, yes. Not like I’m sitting there making plans or anything—I don’t want to hurt myself. But the thought has been coming up… like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.\n\nAnd then I get scared that I even thought it, honestly. That’s part of why I finally made this appointment. I kept telling myself it was just stress, like when inventory gets backed up and everyone’s asking me where things are, and you just want to disappear for five minutes—but this felt different. It kept coming back.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these thoughts about not waking up come to you?\n\nPatient: It comes up… regularly, I guess. Not every minute or anything, and I’m not making plans to hurt myself, but it’s enough that it scared me.\n\nIt’ll be this thought like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And then I think, why did I even think that? You know, I’ll be doing something ordinary, like getting ready for work or sitting after dinner, and it sort of drifts in. That’s really what finally pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think both, actually, which sounds strange when I say it.\n\nSome days I catch myself pacing around the living room, kind of going back and forth for no real reason, and picking at my cuticles. I’ll realize I’ve been doing it while the TV is on, but I haven’t watched a single thing. My sister used to do that during football games—well, not the cuticles, she’d tap her foot so hard it drove everyone crazy—but anyway.\n\nOther days it’s the opposite. Like at work, walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels… weirdly slow, like my body just won’t move at a normal pace. Not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in how your clothes fit over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, my jeans are always a little different depending on the brand—don’t get me started on women’s sizing, it makes no sense—but I haven’t noticed anything major, like suddenly needing a different size.\n\nMaybe a little tighter or looser here and there, but nothing I’d call significant over the last six months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety at times?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’d still go sometimes, but it didn’t feel restorative, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed in any big way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, though. I used to keep a scale in the bathroom, but then it started giving weird numbers after my niece’s kids were jumping around in there one weekend—well, they weren’t on the scale exactly, but you know how kids are. So I can’t say for certain. But significant weight loss? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not waking up more than once or twice, or have they been coming up regularly?\n\nPatient: It’s been more than once or twice, yes. Not like I’m sitting there making plans or anything—I don’t want to hurt myself. But the thought has been coming up… like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.\n\nAnd then I get scared that I even thought it, honestly. That’s part of why I finally made this appointment. I kept telling myself it was just stress, like when inventory gets backed up and everyone’s asking me where things are, and you just want to disappear for five minutes—but this felt different. It kept coming back.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these thoughts about not waking up come to you?\n\nPatient: It comes up… regularly, I guess. Not every minute or anything, and I’m not making plans to hurt myself, but it’s enough that it scared me.\n\nIt’ll be this thought like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And then I think, why did I even think that? You know, I’ll be doing something ordinary, like getting ready for work or sitting after dinner, and it sort of drifts in. That’s really what finally pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think both, actually, which sounds strange when I say it.\n\nSome days I catch myself pacing around the living room, kind of going back and forth for no real reason, and picking at my cuticles. I’ll realize I’ve been doing it while the TV is on, but I haven’t watched a single thing. My sister used to do that during football games—well, not the cuticles, she’d tap her foot so hard it drove everyone crazy—but anyway.\n\nOther days it’s the opposite. Like at work, walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels… weirdly slow, like my body just won’t move at a normal pace. Not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in how your clothes fit over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, my jeans are always a little different depending on the brand—don’t get me started on women’s sizing, it makes no sense—but I haven’t noticed anything major, like suddenly needing a different size.\n\nMaybe a little tighter or looser here and there, but nothing I’d call significant over the last six months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety at times?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some days I’ll realize I’ve been pacing around the living room, just back and forth, and picking at my cuticles without really meaning to.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to get annoyed at people who did that kind of thing in meetings—there was this woman at work who clicked her pen constantly, drove everyone nuts—but now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nAnd then other days it’s the opposite, like even walking from the break room back to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like I’m dragging myself. So yes… restless sometimes, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Definitely more than two weeks. It’s been more like… I don’t know, about six months of this low, flat feeling.\n\nI used to have things that kind of reset me, like my art class, or taking a walk in the morning—there’s this one route with a house that always has ridiculous seasonal decorations, even for tiny holidays, which used to make me smile—but lately those things just don’t do much. I still know they’re supposed to help, but they don’t really feel restorative the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—my scale is one of those digital ones that gives a different number if you shift your foot, which is annoying—but I haven’t noticed my clothes hanging off me or anything like that.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a noticeable amount, at least not that I can tell.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I still try—I’ll set out my paints at home, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because I always liked that. But then I get there and I just sort of… stare at the clay, or the brushes, and I don’t feel that spark anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to lose track of time with that stuff. It’s hard to pin down exactly, because at first I kept blaming it on being busy at the store or the weather, you know, when it was too hot to take my morning walk and then suddenly it was raining every other day.\n\nBut around six months is when I noticed the flatness wasn’t really lifting. The art classes, the walks, those little routines that usually made me feel like myself… they just stopped doing that. I’d still go sometimes, but it didn’t feel restorative, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed in any big way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, though. I used to keep a scale in the bathroom, but then it started giving weird numbers after my niece’s kids were jumping around in there one weekend—well, they weren’t on the scale exactly, but you know how kids are. So I can’t say for certain. But significant weight loss? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not waking up more than once or twice, or have they been coming up regularly?\n\nPatient: It’s been more than once or twice, yes. Not like I’m sitting there making plans or anything—I don’t want to hurt myself. But the thought has been coming up… like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.\n\nAnd then I get scared that I even thought it, honestly. That’s part of why I finally made this appointment. I kept telling myself it was just stress, like when inventory gets backed up and everyone’s asking me where things are, and you just want to disappear for five minutes—but this felt different. It kept coming back.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these thoughts about not waking up come to you?\n\nPatient: It comes up… regularly, I guess. Not every minute or anything, and I’m not making plans to hurt myself, but it’s enough that it scared me.\n\nIt’ll be this thought like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And then I think, why did I even think that? You know, I’ll be doing something ordinary, like getting ready for work or sitting after dinner, and it sort of drifts in. That’s really what finally pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think both, actually, which sounds strange when I say it.\n\nSome days I catch myself pacing around the living room, kind of going back and forth for no real reason, and picking at my cuticles. I’ll realize I’ve been doing it while the TV is on, but I haven’t watched a single thing. My sister used to do that during football games—well, not the cuticles, she’d tap her foot so hard it drove everyone crazy—but anyway.\n\nOther days it’s the opposite. Like at work, walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels… weirdly slow, like my body just won’t move at a normal pace. Not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in how your clothes fit over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, my jeans are always a little different depending on the brand—don’t get me started on women’s sizing, it makes no sense—but I haven’t noticed anything major, like suddenly needing a different size.\n\nMaybe a little tighter or looser here and there, but nothing I’d call significant over the last six months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety at times?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some days I’ll realize I’ve been pacing around the living room, just back and forth, and picking at my cuticles without really meaning to.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to get annoyed at people who did that kind of thing in meetings—there was this woman at work who clicked her pen constantly, drove everyone nuts—but now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nAnd then other days it’s the opposite, like even walking from the break room back to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like I’m dragging myself. So yes… restless sometimes, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Definitely more than two weeks. It’s been more like… I don’t know, about six months of this low, flat feeling.\n\nI used to have things that kind of reset me, like my art class, or taking a walk in the morning—there’s this one route with a house that always has ridiculous seasonal decorations, even for tiny holidays, which used to make me smile—but lately those things just don’t do much. I still know they’re supposed to help, but they don’t really feel restorative the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Not every minute or anything, and I’m not making plans to hurt myself, but it’s enough that it scared me.\n\nIt’ll be this thought like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. I still know they’re supposed to help, but they don’t really feel restorative the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment was… I’ve been having these thoughts, like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. I don’t mean I want to hurt myself—because I don’t—but it scared me that my mind even goes there.\n\nI almost didn’t call, actually. I kept telling myself, “Oh, you’re just tired,” and then I’d get busy with work stuff, schedules and customers and all that, and put it off. But it’s been sitting with me, and I thought I should probably talk to someone before I just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: I’m not exactly sure when it started in a clear way. It’s more that it’s been creeping in lately, like I’ll be lying there or just worn out after a day, and the thought pops up—if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nI don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t. That’s part of why it scared me. I’m usually the one keeping things moving, you know, at work and everything, and then to have that thought show up… it made me think I shouldn’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still try—I’ll set out my paints at home, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because I always liked that. But then I get there and I just sort of… stare at the clay, or the brushes, and I don’t feel that spark anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to lose track of time with that stuff. And now I’ll start thinking about errands or whether I forgot to schedule someone properly at the store, and then suddenly I’m just sitting there doing nothing. So yes, I guess I’m not really enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem about the same, I guess.\n\nThough, honestly, I avoid the scale sometimes because then I start thinking about everything else I should be doing—meal planning, walking more, all of that—and then I end up reorganizing a drawer or making a list for the store schedule instead. But no, nothing I’ve really noticed weight-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any real way. I go to bed, and then sometimes I start thinking about what I didn’t finish around the house or little things from work, but I don’t know if that counts as “trouble sleeping” or just… being me.\n\nI guess I’d have to pay more attention to it. I can’t really say for sure right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep thinking I will, like with painting or pottery. I’ll even set out my paints sometimes, or tell myself, “Go to the class, you always liked the class.” But then I’m there and I just sort of look at the clay or the brushes, and nothing really… catches.\n\nI used to get so absorbed in it. I’d forget to eat lunch if I was working on something, which drove my sister crazy because she’d say I was impossible to make plans with. Now it’s more like I’m going through the motions. So I guess there isn’t much I really look forward to the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, over little things mostly. Like at work, if a display sign is a little crooked, or one of the trainees looks confused after I’ve explained something, I’ll keep replaying it later.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, “What kind of supervisor am I if I can’t even make that clear?” It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because it’s not like the store fell apart or anything. But I can get stuck on it and start telling myself I’m just… useless at my job.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with these thoughts and lack of enjoyment in activities?\n\nPatient: I think… about six months? It’s hard to pin down exactly, because at first I kept blaming it on being busy at the store or the weather, you know, when it was too hot to take my morning walk and then suddenly it was raining every other day.\n\nBut around six months is when I noticed the flatness wasn’t really lifting. The art classes, the walks, those little routines that usually made me feel like myself… they just stopped doing that. I’d still go sometimes, but it didn’t feel restorative, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed in any big way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, though. I used to keep a scale in the bathroom, but then it started giving weird numbers after my niece’s kids were jumping around in there one weekend—well, they weren’t on the scale exactly, but you know how kids are. So I can’t say for certain. But significant weight loss? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not waking up more than once or twice, or have they been coming up regularly?\n\nPatient: It’s been more than once or twice, yes. Not like I’m sitting there making plans or anything—I don’t want to hurt myself. But the thought has been coming up… like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.\n\nAnd then I get scared that I even thought it, honestly. That’s part of why I finally made this appointment. I kept telling myself it was just stress, like when inventory gets backed up and everyone’s asking me where things are, and you just want to disappear for five minutes—but this felt different. It kept coming back.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these thoughts about not waking up come to you?\n\nPatient: It comes up… regularly, I guess. Not every minute or anything, and I’m not making plans to hurt myself, but it’s enough that it scared me.\n\nIt’ll be this thought like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And then I think, why did I even think that? You know, I’ll be doing something ordinary, like getting ready for work or sitting after dinner, and it sort of drifts in. That’s really what finally pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think both, actually, which sounds strange when I say it.\n\nSome days I catch myself pacing around the living room, kind of going back and forth for no real reason, and picking at my cuticles. I’ll realize I’ve been doing it while the TV is on, but I haven’t watched a single thing. My sister used to do that during football games—well, not the cuticles, she’d tap her foot so hard it drove everyone crazy—but anyway.\n\nOther days it’s the opposite. Like at work, walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels… weirdly slow, like my body just won’t move at a normal pace. Not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in how your clothes fit over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, my jeans are always a little different depending on the brand—don’t get me started on women’s sizing, it makes no sense—but I haven’t noticed anything major, like suddenly needing a different size.\n\nMaybe a little tighter or looser here and there, but nothing I’d call significant over the last six months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety at times?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some days I’ll realize I’ve been pacing around the living room, just back and forth, and picking at my cuticles without really meaning to.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to get annoyed at people who did that kind of thing in meetings—there was this woman at work who clicked her pen constantly, drove everyone nuts—but now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nAnd then other days it’s the opposite, like even walking from the break room back to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like I’m dragging myself. So yes… restless sometimes, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Definitely more than two weeks. It’s been more like… I don’t know, about six months of this low, flat feeling.\n\nI used to have things that kind of reset me, like my art class, or taking a walk in the morning—there’s this one route with a house that always has ridiculous seasonal decorations, even for tiny holidays, which used to make me smile—but lately those things just don’t do much. I still know they’re supposed to help, but they don’t really feel restorative the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—my scale is one of those digital ones that gives a different number if you shift your foot, which is annoying—but I haven’t noticed my clothes hanging off me or anything like that.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a noticeable amount, at least not that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight unintentionally over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not unintentionally in any big way. I mean, I said I haven’t really been tracking it closely, and honestly I probably should—my sister has this whole system with a notebook and everything, which feels very organized in a way I am not.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything major, like my clothes suddenly being loose or people commenting on it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Have you had anyone comment on your weight loss over the past six months?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. My sister would probably say something if she noticed—she notices everything, even if my curtains are a little crooked when she comes over. And at work, people comment on all kinds of things, but nobody’s mentioned my weight. So… no, I don’t think anyone has.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of interest or pleasure in most activities for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I don’t try, though. I’ll still set out my paints, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to pottery class—there’s this one woman there who makes these little bird-shaped bowls, which sounds odd but they’re actually really charming—and I’ll get there and just… sit there.\n\nI look at the materials and I know I used to feel excited, like I’d have ideas right away, but lately it’s just kind of blank. The spark isn’t there the way it used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

The spark isn’t there the way it used to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nFor how long have you felt this lack of interest or pleasure in most activities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say… about six months, give or take. It kind of settled in slowly, not like one morning I woke up and everything was gray.

But for around six months now, that low, flat feeling has been there enough that the things that usually help—like art class, or even my morning walks—just don’t feel like they refill the tank the way they used to. I still do some of them, or I mean to, but they don’t really feel restorative anymore.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment was… I’ve been having these thoughts, like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. I don’t mean I want to hurt myself—because I don’t—but it scared me that my mind even goes there.\n\nI almost didn’t call, actually. I kept telling myself, “Oh, you’re just tired,” and then I’d get busy with work stuff, schedules and customers and all that, and put it off. But it’s been sitting with me, and I thought I should probably talk to someone before I just keep brushing it aside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: I’m not exactly sure when it started in a clear way. It’s more that it’s been creeping in lately, like I’ll be lying there or just worn out after a day, and the thought pops up—if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nI don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t. That’s part of why it scared me. I’m usually the one keeping things moving, you know, at work and everything, and then to have that thought show up… it made me think I shouldn’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still try—I’ll set out my paints at home, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because I always liked that. But then I get there and I just sort of… stare at the clay, or the brushes, and I don’t feel that spark anymore.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to lose track of time with that stuff. And now I’ll start thinking about errands or whether I forgot to schedule someone properly at the store, and then suddenly I’m just sitting there doing nothing. So yes, I guess I’m not really enjoying those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t really been tracking it closely. My clothes seem about the same, I guess.\n\nThough, honestly, I avoid the scale sometimes because then I start thinking about everything else I should be doing—meal planning, walking more, all of that—and then I end up reorganizing a drawer or making a list for the store schedule instead. But no, nothing I’ve really noticed weight-wise.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any real way. I go to bed, and then sometimes I start thinking about what I didn’t finish around the house or little things from work, but I don’t know if that counts as “trouble sleeping” or just… being me.\n\nI guess I’d have to pay more attention to it. I can’t really say for sure right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep thinking I will, like with painting or pottery. I’ll even set out my paints sometimes, or tell myself, “Go to the class, you always liked the class.” But then I’m there and I just sort of look at the clay or the brushes, and nothing really… catches.\n\nI used to get so absorbed in it. I’d forget to eat lunch if I was working on something, which drove my sister crazy because she’d say I was impossible to make plans with. Now it’s more like I’m going through the motions. So I guess there isn’t much I really look forward to the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, over little things mostly. Like at work, if a display sign is a little crooked, or one of the trainees looks confused after I’ve explained something, I’ll keep replaying it later.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, “What kind of supervisor am I if I can’t even make that clear?” It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because it’s not like the store fell apart or anything. But I can get stuck on it and start telling myself I’m just… useless at my job.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with these thoughts and lack of enjoyment in activities?\n\nPatient: I think… about six months? It’s hard to pin down exactly, because at first I kept blaming it on being busy at the store or the weather, you know, when it was too hot to take my morning walk and then suddenly it was raining every other day.\n\nBut around six months is when I noticed the flatness wasn’t really lifting. The art classes, the walks, those little routines that usually made me feel like myself… they just stopped doing that. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI don’t weigh myself very regularly, though. I used to keep a scale in the bathroom, but then it started giving weird numbers after my niece’s kids were jumping around in there one weekend—well, they weren’t on the scale exactly, but you know how kids are. So I can’t say for certain. But significant weight loss? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts about not waking up more than once or twice, or have they been coming up regularly?\n\nPatient: It’s been more than once or twice, yes. Not like I’m sitting there making plans or anything—I don’t want to hurt myself. But the thought has been coming up… like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.\n\nAnd then I get scared that I even thought it, honestly. That’s part of why I finally made this appointment. I kept telling myself it was just stress, like when inventory gets backed up and everyone’s asking me where things are, and you just want to disappear for five minutes—but this felt different. It kept coming back.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say these thoughts about not waking up come to you?\n\nPatient: It comes up… regularly, I guess. Not every minute or anything, and I’m not making plans to hurt myself, but it’s enough that it scared me.\n\nIt’ll be this thought like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And then I think, why did I even think that? You know, I’ll be doing something ordinary, like getting ready for work or sitting after dinner, and it sort of drifts in. That’s really what finally pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think both, actually, which sounds strange when I say it.\n\nSome days I catch myself pacing around the living room, kind of going back and forth for no real reason, and picking at my cuticles. I’ll realize I’ve been doing it while the TV is on, but I haven’t watched a single thing. My sister used to do that during football games—well, not the cuticles, she’d tap her foot so hard it drove everyone crazy—but anyway.\n\nOther days it’s the opposite. Like at work, walking from the break room back out to the sales floor feels… weirdly slow, like my body just won’t move at a normal pace. Not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in how your clothes fit over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, my jeans are always a little different depending on the brand—don’t get me started on women’s sizing, it makes no sense—but I haven’t noticed anything major, like suddenly needing a different size.\n\nMaybe a little tighter or looser here and there, but nothing I’d call significant over the last six months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety at times?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Some days I’ll realize I’ve been pacing around the living room, just back and forth, and picking at my cuticles without really meaning to.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to get annoyed at people who did that kind of thing in meetings—there was this woman at work who clicked her pen constantly, drove everyone nuts—but now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nAnd then other days it’s the opposite, like even walking from the break room back to the sales floor feels strangely slow, like I’m dragging myself. So yes… restless sometimes, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two weeks or more where you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Definitely more than two weeks. It’s been more like… I don’t know, about six months of this low, flat feeling.\n\nI used to have things that kind of reset me, like my art class, or taking a walk in the morning—there’s this one route with a house that always has ridiculous seasonal decorations, even for tiny holidays, which used to make me smile—but lately those things just don’t do much. I still know they’re supposed to help, but they don’t really feel restorative the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—my scale is one of those digital ones that gives a different number if you shift your foot, which is annoying—but I haven’t noticed my clothes hanging off me or anything like that.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a noticeable amount, at least not that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight unintentionally over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not unintentionally in any big way. I mean, I said I haven’t really been tracking it closely, and honestly I probably should—my sister has this whole system with a notebook and everything, which feels very organized in a way I am not.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything major, like my clothes suddenly being loose or people commenting on it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Have you had anyone comment on your weight loss over the past six months?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. It’s been more like… I don’t know, about six months of this low, flat feeling.\n\nI used to have things that kind of reset me, like my art class, or taking a walk in the morning—there’s this one route with a house that always has ridiculous seasonal decorations, even for tiny holidays, which used to make me smile—but lately those things just don’t do much. I still know they’re supposed to help, but they don’t really feel restorative the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly—my scale is one of those digital ones that gives a different number if you shift your foot, which is annoying—but I haven’t noticed my clothes hanging off me or anything like that.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a noticeable amount, at least not that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight unintentionally over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not unintentionally in any big way. I mean, I said I haven’t really been tracking it closely, and honestly I probably should—my sister has this whole system with a notebook and everything, which feels very organized in a way I am not.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed anything major, like my clothes suddenly being loose or people commenting on it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a significant amount.\n\nDoctor: Have you had anyone comment on your weight loss over the past six months?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. My sister would probably say something if she noticed—she notices everything, even if my curtains are a little crooked when she comes over. And at work, people comment on all kinds of things, but nobody’s mentioned my weight. So… no, I don’t think anyone has.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of interest or pleasure in most activities for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I don’t try, though. I’ll still set out my paints, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to pottery class—there’s this one woman there who makes these little bird-shaped bowls, which sounds odd but they’re actually really charming—and I’ll get there and just… sit there.\n\nI look at the materials and I know I used to feel excited, like I’d have ideas right away, but lately it’s just kind of blank. The spark isn’t there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you felt this lack of interest or pleasure in most activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about six months, give or take. It kind of settled in slowly, not like one morning I woke up and everything was gray.\n\nBut for around six months now, that low, flat feeling has been there enough that the things that usually help—like art class, or even my morning walks—just don’t feel like they refill the tank the way they used to. I still do some of them, or I mean to, but they don’t really feel restorative anymore."
